People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even to small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but at work if the store alarm beeps or a pallet jack bangs behind me, I jump really hard—like I almost drop whatever I’m holding.\n\nAnd then my heart keeps pounding for a while after, even when everyone else has already gone back to normal. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire or make you feel anxious?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / retail_salesperson / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been avoiding the house, the mortgage folder, and even the herbs on my patio because anything tied to that day makes my chest tighten and I just can’t make myself go near it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Um… yes, I guess I have been avoiding some things. I don’t want to sound dramatic, but I haven’t really been going near the house much if I can help it.

Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire or make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, I guess I have been avoiding some things. I’ll be doing something normal, like picking up a pan or even opening a spreadsheet at work, and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there—smelling smoke, seeing the kitchen filling up—and I can’t really stop it right away.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yeah, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific to make these intrusive thoughts or memories go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might try to distract myself or tell myself to stop thinking about it, but I don’t have a specific thing I feel like I have to do. At least, I don’t think I do. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming need to perform a specific behavior to reduce anxiety or distress associated with these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I do get anxious when the thoughts come up, but I don’t feel like there’s some specific thing I have to do to make it better.\n\nI might take a breath or try to focus on something else, but it’s not like I have a rule or routine I have to follow. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have been, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I’ve been staying away from the house as much as I can. Even things like the mortgage folder—I just can’t bring myself to open it.\n\nAnd this sounds silly, but even the herbs on my patio… they remind me of that day, and my chest just tightens up. I know it’s probably not rational, but anything tied to the fire feels like too much right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two weeks now. Since the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nIt feels like it started right after that, but I don’t know if I’m explaining the timing clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’m constantly looking for danger, exactly. I mean, I’m a little jumpy sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed myself scanning for threats or anything like that.\n\nMaybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even to small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but at work if the store alarm beeps or a pallet jack bangs behind me, I jump really hard—like I almost drop whatever I’m holding.\n\nAnd then my heart keeps pounding for a while after, even when everyone else has already gone back to normal. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire or make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, I guess I have been avoiding some things. I don’t want to sound dramatic, but I haven’t really been going near the house much if I can help it.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, but even seeing it makes my chest tighten, so I just… don’t open it. Even the herbs out on my patio, which sounds silly, I know, but they remind me of that day and I just can’t make myself go near them.\n\nIs that the kind of avoidance you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments where you felt disconnected from yourself or your surroundings since the fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “disconnected” exactly, but sometimes when I’m walking through the store, it feels like I’m sort of watching myself from a few feet away.\n\nLike the aisles and the customers are there, but they don’t feel quite real, and I’m just moving through everything on automatic. It’s strange, and I haven’t really wanted to say it because it sounds… weird. But is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other moments where you felt like you were outside of your body or watching yourself, similar to what you described happening at the store?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it’s mostly like what I said with the store. I’ll be walking through the aisles or helping customers, and suddenly it feels like I’m not fully there—like I’m watching myself from a little distance.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, and I can still do what I’m supposed to do, so maybe it’s not that serious… but things around me feel kind of unreal, and I’m just moving through it on autopilot. Is that… the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nIt’s like the fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like picking up a pan or even opening a spreadsheet at work, and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there—smelling smoke, seeing the kitchen filling up—and I can’t really stop it right away.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yeah, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific to make these intrusive thoughts or memories go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might try to distract myself or tell myself to stop thinking about it, but I don’t have a specific thing I feel like I have to do. I mean, more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nIt’s like the fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like picking up a pan or even opening a spreadsheet at work, and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there—smelling smoke, seeing the kitchen filling up—and I can’t really stop it right away.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yeah, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific to make these intrusive thoughts or memories go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might try to distract myself or tell myself to stop thinking about it, but I don’t have a specific thing I feel like I have to do. I mean… I do get anxious when the thoughts come up, but I don’t feel like there’s some specific thing I have to do to make it better.\n\nI might take a breath or try to focus on something else, but it’s not like I have a rule or routine I have to follow. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have been, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I’ve been staying away from the house as much as I can. Even things like the mortgage folder—I just can’t bring myself to open it.\n\nAnd this sounds silly, but even the herbs on my patio… they remind me of that day, and my chest just tightens up. I know it’s probably not rational, but anything tied to the fire feels like too much right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two weeks now. Since the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nIt feels like it started right after that, but I don’t know if I’m explaining the timing clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’m constantly looking for danger, exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even to small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Is that… the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire that happened?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I just… thought I should maybe talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or urges that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, a little. I mean, more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nIt’s like the fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like picking up a pan or even opening a spreadsheet at work, and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there—smelling smoke, seeing the kitchen filling up—and I can’t really stop it right away.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yeah, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific to make these intrusive thoughts or memories go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might try to distract myself or tell myself to stop thinking about it, but I don’t have a specific thing I feel like I have to do. I mean… I do get anxious when the thoughts come up, but I don’t feel like there’s some specific thing I have to do to make it better.\n\nI might take a breath or try to focus on something else, but it’s not like I have a rule or routine I have to follow. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have been, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I’ve been staying away from the house as much as I can. Even things like the mortgage folder—I just can’t bring myself to open it.\n\nAnd this sounds silly, but even the herbs on my patio… they remind me of that day, and my chest just tightens up. I know it’s probably not rational, but anything tied to the fire feels like too much right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two weeks now. Since the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nIt feels like it started right after that, but I don’t know if I’m explaining the timing clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’m constantly looking for danger, exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even to small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange, and I haven’t really wanted to say it because it sounds… weird. I’ll be walking through the aisles or helping customers, and suddenly it feels like I’m not fully there—like I’m watching myself from a little distance.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, and I can still do what I’m supposed to do, so maybe it’s not that serious… but things around me feel kind of unreal, and I’m just moving through it on autopilot. Is that… the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire that happened?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve just been a little more on edge than usual. I don’t normally lose my temper, but lately I keep snapping at people over things that aren’t really a big deal.\n\nThe other day at work, I yelled at one of my employees because they put stock on the wrong shelf, and afterward I was actually shaking. I felt terrible, because that’s really not how I usually treat people. I just… thought I should maybe talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or urges that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, a little. I mean, more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nIt’s like the fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like picking up a pan or even opening a spreadsheet at work, and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there—smelling smoke, seeing the kitchen filling up—and I can’t really stop it right away.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yeah, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific to make these intrusive thoughts or memories go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might try to distract myself or tell myself to stop thinking about it, but I don’t have a specific thing I feel like I have to do. At least, I don’t think I do. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming need to perform a specific behavior to reduce anxiety or distress associated with these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I do get anxious when the thoughts come up, but I don’t feel like there’s some specific thing I have to do to make it better.\n\nI might take a breath or try to focus on something else, but it’s not like I have a rule or routine I have to follow. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have been, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I’ve been staying away from the house as much as I can. Even things like the mortgage folder—I just can’t bring myself to open it.\n\nAnd this sounds silly, but even the herbs on my patio… they remind me of that day, and my chest just tightens up. I know it’s probably not rational, but anything tied to the fire feels like too much right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two weeks now. Since the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nIt feels like it started right after that, but I don’t know if I’m explaining the timing clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’m constantly looking for danger, exactly. I mean, I’m a little jumpy sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed myself scanning for threats or anything like that.\n\nMaybe I’m not paying close enough attention to it, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even to small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but at work if the store alarm beeps or a pallet jack bangs behind me, I jump really hard—like I almost drop whatever I’m holding.\n\nAnd then my heart keeps pounding for a while after, even when everyone else has already gone back to normal. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire or make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, I guess I have been avoiding some things. I don’t want to sound dramatic, but I haven’t really been going near the house much if I can help it.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, but even seeing it makes my chest tighten, so I just… don’t open it. Even the herbs out on my patio, which sounds silly, I know, but they remind me of that day and I just can’t make myself go near them.\n\nIs that the kind of avoidance you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments where you felt disconnected from yourself or your surroundings since the fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “disconnected” exactly, but sometimes when I’m walking through the store, it feels like I’m sort of watching myself from a few feet away.\n\nLike the aisles and the customers are there, but they don’t feel quite real, and I’m just moving through everything on automatic. It’s strange, and I haven’t really wanted to say it because it sounds… weird. But is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other moments where you felt like you were outside of your body or watching yourself, similar to what you described happening at the store?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it’s mostly like what I said with the store. I’ll be walking through the aisles or helping customers, and suddenly it feels like I’m not fully there—like I’m watching myself from a little distance.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, and I can still do what I’m supposed to do, so maybe it’s not that serious… but things around me feel kind of unreal, and I’m just moving through it on autopilot. Is that… the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire that happened?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I mean, I know accidents happen, and I’m trying not to make it into something bigger than it is.\n\nThere are moments where I wonder if I should’ve noticed something sooner or done something differently, but I’m not sure if that’s really guilt or just… replaying it. I don’t want to blame anyone, or make it sound like I’m saying it was all my fault.\n\nIs that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in activities that you normally enjoy since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I still try to do the normal things when I can. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore,” or anything like that.\n\nMaybe I’ve been a little more distracted, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m less interested exactly. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in how much you enjoy the things you used to like doing before the fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve really noticed that, no. I mean… I haven’t been doing everything the same, but I wouldn’t say the enjoyment is gone exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just trying to keep things normal, if that makes sense. I still want to like the things I usually like. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you normally like to do since the fire happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I know that probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but… I still want to do the things I normally like.\n\nMaybe I’m not as settled when I’m doing them, but the interest itself doesn’t feel gone. I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your relationships with others since the fire happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I know that’s not great.\n\nAnd at home, I still haven’t been able to make myself go back to the damaged house, so things are piling up there too. Responsibilities, decisions, all of it. I don’t want to sound like I’m falling apart, but it’s getting ahead of me faster than I can handle. Does that count as problems?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with family or friends have been affected by how you've been feeling since the fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to sound dramatic, but I haven’t really been going near the house much if I can help it.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, but even seeing it makes my chest tighten, so I just… don’t open it. Even the herbs out on my patio, which sounds silly, I know, but they remind me of that day and I just can’t make myself go near them.\n\nIs that the kind of avoidance you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments where you felt disconnected from yourself or your surroundings since the fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “disconnected” exactly, but sometimes when I’m walking through the store, it feels like I’m sort of watching myself from a few feet away.\n\nLike the aisles and the customers are there, but they don’t feel quite real, and I’m just moving through everything on automatic. It’s strange, and I haven’t really wanted to say it because it sounds… weird. But is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other moments where you felt like you were outside of your body or watching yourself, similar to what you described happening at the store?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it’s mostly like what I said with the store. I’ll be walking through the aisles or helping customers, and suddenly it feels like I’m not fully there—like I’m watching myself from a little distance.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, and I can still do what I’m supposed to do, so maybe it’s not that serious… but things around me feel kind of unreal, and I’m just moving through it on autopilot. Is that… the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire that happened?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I mean, I know accidents happen, and I’m trying not to make it into something bigger than it is.\n\nThere are moments where I wonder if I should’ve noticed something sooner or done something differently, but I’m not sure if that’s really guilt or just… replaying it. I don’t want to blame anyone, or make it sound like I’m saying it was all my fault.\n\nIs that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in activities that you normally enjoy since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I still try to do the normal things when I can. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore,” or anything like that.\n\nMaybe I’ve been a little more distracted, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m less interested exactly. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in how much you enjoy the things you used to like doing before the fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve really noticed that, no. I mean… I haven’t been doing everything the same, but I wouldn’t say the enjoyment is gone exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just trying to keep things normal, if that makes sense. I still want to like the things I usually like. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you normally like to do since the fire happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I know that probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but… I still want to do the things I normally like.\n\nMaybe I’m not as settled when I’m doing them, but the interest itself doesn’t feel gone. I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your relationships with others since the fire happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m trying not to make excuses, but work has been harder than usual. I missed a vendor meeting, and there was one shift where I just kind of… left before I finished. I know that’s not great.\n\nAnd at home, I still haven’t been able to make myself go back to the damaged house, so things are piling up there too. Responsibilities, decisions, all of it. I don’t want to sound like I’m falling apart, but it’s getting ahead of me faster than I can handle. Does that count as problems?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with family or friends have been affected by how you've been feeling since the fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I hate saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m taking it out on people on purpose.\n\nI’ve been more short with people than I usually am. Like at work, I yelled at one of my employees for putting stock on the wrong shelf, and it was such a small thing. Afterward I was shaking, because… that’s not me. I don’t usually treat people that way.\n\nSo yes, I guess it has affected people around me. I’m trying to keep it together, but I know I haven’t been as patient. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't enjoy things as much as you used to since the fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean… things don’t feel normal, but I don’t think it’s that I don’t enjoy them anymore.\n\nIf I can actually get myself to do something I usually like, there are still moments where it feels okay. Maybe it’s just harder to relax into it. I don’t know if that makes sense, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the house and other things that remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I think. Since the kitchen fire… it was during our first weekend working on the house, and I just haven’t really been able to face it since then.\n\nI know two weeks maybe doesn’t sound that long, but it feels like I should be handling it better by now."
